Remark 19 (Scope of the perturbation class) . The two sequences P ± 

> n

of Theorem 4 are of the form 

Pn,u,s with s = ˜ s and u = ±h/b , so the class of the theorem contains them once Bu ≥ h/ (σK0 − ε).Total boundedness of S in L2(P0) holds, for example, for any finite collection of scores, for parametric families indexed by compact sets and continuous in the index, and for uniformly bounded classes with finite bracketing entropy. The boundedness of the scores matches the least favorable construction in the impossibility proof, which is itself built from a truncated influence function, so no power is lost at the root-n scale by the restriction. 

Remark 20 (Cardinality along the drifting sequences) . Steps 3 to 6 of the proof also bound the size of the report along the same sequences. Fix γ = ρ0(K0) as in Theorem 4. Under Qn, every K < K 0 has 

ˆρ(K) p

→ ρ0(K) < γ by Step 4 while the band radius q1−α ˆσ(K) is of order n−1/2 by Step 5, so Uρ(K) < γ 

with probability tending to one and every such K leaves the report. Likewise Lρ(K0 +1) p

→ ρ0(K0 +1) > γ ,so the defining condition of (16) eventually excludes every K ≥ K0 + 2 . Hence Qn{̂ C(γ) ⊆ { K0, K 0 +1}} → 1, and the honest report costs at most the two knot-adjacent counts, matching the mean cardinality near two observed in Study 4. 

48 S6.11 Proofs for the penalized profile and atomic recovery 

Throughout this subsection, recall W (K) = inf c∈C K Ψ0(gc) and note that W is automatically nonincreas-ing: any K-codebook is a (K + 1) -codebook with a repeated center. Hence every slope ratio appearing in Proposition 1(ii) is nonnegative. 

S6.11.1 Proof of Proposition 1 

Each map τ 7 → W (K) + τ K is affine and increasing, so Q, a minimum of K such maps, is concave, nondecreasing, and piecewise linear with at most K pieces. Two elementary facts organize the rest. 

Supporting slopes. If K ∈ S 0(τ ), then Q(τ ′) ≤ W (K) + τ ′K for all τ ′ with equality at τ , so the affine map with slope K supports the concave Q at τ and K ∈ ∂Q (τ ), the superdifferential. Monotone selection. If τ < τ ′, K ∈ S 0(τ ), K′ ∈ S 0(τ ′), adding the two optimality inequalities W (K) + τ K ≤

W (K′) + τ K ′ and W (K′) + τ ′K′ ≤ W (K) + τ ′K gives (τ ′ − τ )( K − K′) ≥ 0, so K ≥ K′: selections are nonincreasing in τ , uniformly over the choice of selection. (i) At a differentiability point, ∂Q (τ ) = {Q′(τ )}, so every member of the nonempty S0(τ ) equals 

Q′(τ ): S0(τ ) = {Q′(τ )}. Conversely, if S0(τ0) = {K}, the gap min L̸=K {W (L) + τ0L} − { W (K) + τ0K}

is positive and continuous in τ0, so S0 = {K} on a neighborhood, on which Q(·) = W (K) + ( ·)K: every active order is a slope of Q, and the slope of each open linear piece is the unique selection there. The set 

{τ : S0(τ ) = {K}} is open and is an interval: if it contains τ1 < τ 2 and τ ∈ (τ1, τ 2), monotone selection against τ1 and against τ2 squeezes every member of S0(τ ) to equal K. At a kink τ(j) the adjacent slopes are K(j) (right piece) and K(j+1) (left piece), so ∂Q (τ(j)) = [ K(j), K (j+1) ] and S0(τ(j)) ⊆ [K(j), K (j+1) ] by the supporting-slope fact; both endpoints lie in S0(τ(j)) because W (K(j)) + τ K (j) = Q(τ ) on the right piece and both sides are continuous at τ(j), and symmetrically from the left. (ii) K is active iff there is τ > 0 with W (K) + τ K < W (L) + τ L for every L̸ = K, i.e. τ >

{W (K) − W (L)}/(L − K) for every L > K and τ < {W (L) − W (K)}/(K − L) for every L < K ; that is, iff the open interval from max L>K to min L<K of the displayed ratios (conventions max ∅ = 0 , min ∅ = ∞)meets (0 , ∞). Since W is nonincreasing all ratios are nonnegative, so the lower endpoint is ≥ 0 and the interval meets (0 , ∞) exactly when it is nonempty, which is the displayed strict inequality, saying that every chord arriving at (K, W (K)) from the left is steeper than every chord leaving to the right: a strict vertex of the greatest convex minorant. For K = 1 the right side is +∞ and the left side finite, so 1 is always active; if W (K) = W (K − 1) the chord from K − 1 contributes 0 to the minimum, so the strict inequality fails and K is inactive. (iii) Each active order’s persistence set is a nonempty open interval by (i), and by monotone selection the interval of the larger of two active orders lies entirely to the left. At any τ that is not a kink, Q

is differentiable, so by (i) τ belongs to the persistence interval of an active order; hence (0 , ∞) minus the finitely many kinks is the disjoint union of the persistence intervals, ordered with K decreasing in τ .Consecutive active orders K(j) < K (j+1) therefore share a single boundary kink τ ∗, at which, by continuity, both attain Q: W (K(j)) + τ ∗K(j) = W (K(j+1) ) + τ ∗K(j+1) , i.e. τ ∗ = τ(j) of Definition 5; it is strictly positive because activity of K(j+1) requires, by (ii) with L = K(j), that {W (K(j))−W (K(j+1) )}/(K(j+1) −

K(j)) strictly exceed a nonnegative quantity. The persistence interval of K(j) is thus exactly (τ(j), τ (j−1) ),nonempty, which is the strict decrease τ(j) < τ (j−1) ; the remaining clauses are immediate. (iv) For all τ and K, Q(τ ) ≤ W (K) + τ K , so sup τ > 0{Q(τ ) − τ K } ≤ W (K), with equality for K ∈ S 

at any τ in its persistence interval. And restricting the minimum to S changes nothing: off kinks the minimum is attained at an active order by (i), and at kinks by the two adjacent active orders. □

S6.11.4 Proof of Proposition 2 

Fix γ > 0, suppose Var {G0(gc⋆(K(j))) − G0(gc⋆(K(j+1) ))} > 0, and set τn = τ(j) + γn −1/2, ∆K = K(j+1) −

K(j). On a fixed compact neighborhood of τ(j) containing no other merge scale, all orders outside the merging pair are eliminated uniformly, with probability tending to one, by the gap argument in the proof of Corollary 4 above; using W (K(j+1) ) + τ(j)K(j+1) = W (K(j)) + τ(j)K(j),

√n{̂ Q(τn) − Q(τn)} = min {Zn(K(j)), Z n(K(j+1) ) + γ ∆K} + oP(1) ⇝ min {ZK(j) , Z K(j+1) + γ∆K},

which proves the local-drift display and shows explicitly that the pointwise limit changes with the n−1/2

drift γ.For non-tightness, keep a γ > 0 for which 

p1 := P{ZK(j) − ZK(j+1) > γ ∆K + 2 ε} > 0

for some ε > 0, possible by the nondegenerate normality of the displayed difference. Let Xn(τ ) = 

√n{̂ Q(τ ) − Q(τ )} and τm = τ(j) + γm −1/2. For every fixed m, as N → ∞ the price τm is fixed to the right of the merge scale, so the competitor is eliminated and 

XN (τm) = ZN (K(j)) + oP(1) , XN (τN ) = min {ZN (K(j)), Z N (K(j+1) ) + γ∆K} + oP(1) .

Therefore 

lim inf   

> N→∞

P{| XN (τN ) − XN (τm)| > ε } ≥ p1.

Suppose, to the contrary, that Xn were asymptotically tight in ℓ∞(T ′) for a set T ′ containing a right neighborhood of τ(j). The asymptotic-tightness criterion in (van der Vaart and Wellner, 1996, Theo-rem 1.5.7) supplies a semimetric ς′ making T ′ totally bounded and making Xn asymptotically uniformly 

ς′-equicontinuous. Applying that criterion with (ε, p 1/2) gives a radius r > 0 such that pairs with ς′-distance below r have increments larger than ε with limiting probability below p1/2. The preceding display then implies that, for every fixed m, ς′(τN , τ m) ≥ r for all sufficiently large N . Inductively choose 

N1 < N 2 < · · · so that each new τNk is at ς′-distance at least r from all earlier selected points. This constructs an infinite r-separated subset of T ′, contradicting total boundedness. Thus no tight weak limit exists on a right neighborhood. The same argument on the left of the merge scale, with the two active orders interchanged, proves the one-sided statement in general. □

Membership surfaces: β 7 → rh(·; β) ∈ ℓ∞(C) is Hadamard (indeed Fréchet) 53 differentiable by the uniformly bounded β-derivatives of Assumption 3(iii), so a second application of the chain rule transfers the conclusion; joint statements over finite sets of K are coordinatewise. □

Let Φη = {ϕf (·; η) : f ∈ F} for η in the truncated range, with constant envelope ¯F .(a) There exist A < ∞ and v < ∞, depending only on the constants declared at the start of Supplemen-tary Section S6, such that sup Q N (ε ¯F , Φη, L 2(Q)) ≤ (A/ε )v for all ε ∈ (0 , 1] , all finitely supported
